There really hasn't been any break or relief from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months in the past when your mood and interest in things were back to normal, or has this low mood been present most of the time for years?\n\nPatient: Before I was passed over for the promotion eight months ago, my mood and interest in things were completely normal, and I had never felt this way before. Since then, though, these feelings have been completely constant without any periods of relief or return to how I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had changes in sleep, appetite, energy, concentration, or feelings of guilt or worthlessness during this time?\n\nPatient: Yes, my sleep has been awful—I either lie awake for hours replaying work emails or wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning with a heavy anxiety and can't fall back asleep. I've also been overwhelmed by feelings of worthlessness, constantly telling myself I'm a fraud and a disappointment to my husband and colleagues since I missed that promotion.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms begin within about 3 months of the promotion setback and work stress, or did they start later and feel out of proportion to that stressor?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, they started pretty much immediately after I was passed over for the promotion. The changes in my mood and sleep began steadily right after that setback, so it was definitely within that three-month window.

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m Dr. ____. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I don’t enjoy my classes or my art anymore, and the things that used to excite me just feel flat and pointless. I just feel completely drained and disconnected from everything lately.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and did it start after a specific stressful event or life change?\n\nPatient: These feelings have been going on pretty steadily for about eight months now. It started after I was passed over for a tenure-track promotion I had worked really hard for, which happened right around the same time my teaching load and work pressure increased.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of at least two months during these eight months when you felt back to your usual self, or has it been present most of the time without much relief?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any periods where I felt back to my usual self at all. It has been completely constant and heavy every single day since I missed out on the promotion. There really hasn't been any break or relief from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months in the past when your mood and interest in things were back to normal, or has this low mood been present most of the time for years?\n\nPatient: Before I was passed over for the promotion eight months ago, my mood and interest in things were completely normal, and I had never felt this way before. Since then, though, these feelings have been completely constant without any periods of relief or return to how I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had changes in sleep, appetite, energy, concentration, or feelings of guilt or worthlessness during this time?\n\nPatient: Yes, my sleep has been awful—I either lie awake for hours replaying work emails or wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning with a heavy anxiety and can't fall back asleep. I've also been overwhelmed by feelings of worthlessness, constantly telling myself I'm a fraud and a disappointment to my husband and colleagues since I missed that promotion.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms begin within about 3 months of the promotion setback and work stress, or did they start later and feel out of proportion to that stressor?\n\nPatient: Yes, they started pretty much immediately after I was passed over for the promotion. The changes in my mood and sleep began steadily right after that setback, so it was definitely within that three-month window.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life is not worth living, or any thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: On my worst days, especially after a long, unproductive evening at my desk, I do catch myself thinking it would be easier if I just didn't wake up or could quietly slip away. But these are just passive thoughts, and I don't have any plan or intention to actually act on them.\n\nDoctor: Since this started after the promotion setback, have you felt the distress is mainly tied to that stressor and your work situation, or does it feel like a depression that is out of proportion and affecting most areas of your life even when you are not thinking about work?"

It started after I was passed over for a tenure-track promotion I had worked really hard for, which happened right around the same time my teaching load and work pressure increased.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of at least two months during these eight months when you felt back to your usual self, or has it been present most of the time without much relief?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any periods where I felt back to my usual self at all. It has been completely constant and heavy every single day since I missed out on the promotion. There really hasn't been any break or relief from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months in the past when your mood and interest in things were back to normal, or has this low mood been present most of the time for years?\n\nPatient: Before I was passed over for the promotion eight months ago, my mood and interest in things were completely normal, and I had never felt this way before. Since then, though, these feelings have been completely constant without any periods of relief or return to how I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had changes in sleep, appetite, energy, concentration, or feelings of guilt or worthlessness during this time?\n\nPatient: Yes, my sleep has been awful—I either lie awake for hours replaying work emails or wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning with a heavy anxiety and can't fall back asleep. I've also been overwhelmed by feelings of worthlessness, constantly telling myself I'm a fraud and a disappointment to my husband and colleagues since I missed that promotion.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms begin within about 3 months of the promotion setback and work stress, or did they start later and feel out of proportion to that stressor?\n\nPatient: Yes, they started pretty much immediately after I was passed over for the promotion. The changes in my mood and sleep began steadily right after that setback, so it was definitely within that three-month window.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life is not worth living, or any thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: On my worst days, especially after a long, unproductive evening at my desk, I do catch myself thinking it would be easier if I just didn't wake up or could quietly slip away. But these are just passive thoughts, and I don't have any plan or intention to actually act on them.\n\nDoctor: Since this started after the promotion setback, have you felt the distress is mainly tied to that stressor and your work situation, or does it feel like a depression that is out of proportion and affecting most areas of your life even when you are not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: It definitely feels like a heavy, constant depression that has spread far beyond my work. Even when I'm not thinking about my job, it affects almost every area of my life, like my passion for art and my marriage, where my husband has had to take over most of the household chores. It just feels like a gray film is over everything now, regardless of where I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks during this time when you felt mostly back to normal, or has the low mood and loss of interest been present nearly every day without relief?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had even a two-week break where I felt back to normal. These feelings of sadness and loss of interest have been constant nearly every single day for the past eight months. There hasn't been any period where I felt like my old self.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least a few days where you felt unusually energized or euphoric, needed much less sleep, talked more than usual, or were more impulsive than normal?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced periods of feeling unusually energized, euphoric, or needing less sleep. My energy has generally been normal in the past, and lately, it has been quite low."

But these are just passive thoughts, and I don't have any plan or intention to actually act on them.\n\nDoctor: Since this started after the promotion setback, have you felt the distress is mainly tied to that stressor and your work situation, or does it feel like a depression that is out of proportion and affecting most areas of your life even when you are not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: It definitely feels like a heavy, constant depression that has spread far beyond my work. Even when I'm not thinking about my job, it affects almost every area of my life, like my passion for art and my marriage, where my husband has had to take over most of the household chores. It just feels like a gray film is over everything now, regardless of where I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks during this time when you felt mostly back to normal, or has the low mood and loss of interest been present nearly every day without relief?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had even a two-week break where I felt back to normal. These feelings of sadness and loss of interest have been constant nearly every single day for the past eight months. There hasn't been any period where I felt like my old self.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least a few days where you felt unusually energized or euphoric, needed much less sleep, talked more than usual, or were more impulsive than normal?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced periods of feeling unusually energized, euphoric, or needing less sleep. My energy has generally been normal in the past, and lately, it has been quite low.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused a clear decline in your ability to function at work, at home, or socially, beyond what you would expect from the promotion setback alone? Also, do you feel sad or empty most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has caused a major decline—I’ve fallen behind on grading, started avoiding department events and canceling student art activities, and my husband has had to take over most of the chores at home. I also feel a heavy, hollow sadness like a gray film over everything most days, and it doesn't lift even when something objectively good happens.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms begin within 3 months of the promotion setback, and do they feel mainly tied to that stressor—or do they seem more like a depression that affects most areas of your life even when you are not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yes, they started pretty much immediately after the promotion setback eight months ago. While that was definitely the trigger, the depression has now spread to affect almost every part of my life, like my marriage, my art, and my daily mood, rather than just being about my job."

Also, do you feel sad or empty most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has caused a major decline—I’ve fallen behind on grading, started avoiding department events and canceling student art activities, and my husband has had to take over most of the chores at home. I also feel a heavy, hollow sadness like a gray film over everything most days, and it doesn't lift even when something objectively good happens.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms begin within 3 months of the promotion setback, and do they feel mainly tied to that stressor—or do they seem more like a depression that affects most areas of your life even when you are not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yes, they started pretty much immediately after the promotion setback eight months ago. While that was definitely the trigger, the depression has now spread to affect almost every part of my life, like my marriage, my art, and my daily mood, rather than just being about my job.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms felt mainly tied to the promotion setback and work stress, or have they persisted as a broader depression affecting most areas of your life even when you are not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: It definitely feels like a broader depression that has spread to almost every area of my life. Even when I am at home away from work, trying to paint or spending time with my husband, that heavy, flat sadness is always there and just won't lift. It is no longer just about the job setback anymore."

While that was definitely the trigger, the depression has now spread to affect almost every part of my life, like my marriage, my art, and my daily mood, rather than just being about my job.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms felt mainly tied to the promotion setback and work stress, or have they persisted as a broader depression affecting most areas of your life even when you are not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: It definitely feels like a broader depression that has spread to almost every area of my life. Even when I am at home away from work, trying to paint or spending time with my husband, that heavy, flat sadness is always there and just won't lift. It is no longer just about the job setback anymore.\n\nDoctor: Before the promotion setback, did you ever have a similar episode of depression or loss of interest lasting at least two weeks? Also, have the symptoms been out of proportion to the stressor and continued even after any improvement in the work situation?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced an episode of depression or lost interest in things like this before the promotion setback. As for my work situation, it hasn't really improved, so it's hard to say, but these feelings definitely feel far out of proportion to just a job setback now."
  }

These feelings of sadness and loss of interest have been constant nearly every single day for the past eight months. There hasn't been any period where I felt like my old self.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least a few days where you felt unusually energized or euphoric, needed much less sleep, talked more than usual, or were more impulsive than normal?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced periods of feeling unusually energized, euphoric, or needing less sleep. My energy has generally been normal in the past, and lately, it has been quite low.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused a clear decline in your ability to function at work, at home, or socially, beyond what you would expect from the promotion setback alone? Also, do you feel sad or empty most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has caused a major decline—I’ve fallen behind on grading, started avoiding department events and canceling student art activities, and my husband has had to take over most of the chores at home. I also feel a heavy, hollow sadness like a gray film over everything most days, and it doesn't lift even when something objectively good happens.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms begin within 3 months of the promotion setback, and do they feel mainly tied to that stressor—or do they seem more like a depression that affects most areas of your life even when you are not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yes, they started pretty much immediately after the promotion setback eight months ago. While that was definitely the trigger, the depression has now spread to affect almost every part of my life, like my marriage, my art, and my daily mood, rather than just being about my job.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms felt mainly tied to the promotion setback and work stress, or have they persisted as a broader depression affecting most areas of your life even when you are not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: It definitely feels like a broader depression that has spread to almost every area of my life. Even when I am at home away from work, trying to paint or spending time with my husband, that heavy, flat sadness is always there and just won't lift. It is no longer just about the job setback anymore.\n\nDoctor: Before the promotion setback, did you ever have a similar episode of depression or loss of interest lasting at least two weeks? Also, have the symptoms been out of proportion to the stressor and continued even after any improvement in the work situation?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced an episode of depression or lost interest in things like this before the promotion setback. As for my work situation, it hasn't really improved, so it's hard to say, but these feelings definitely feel far out of proportion to just a job setback now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other major stressful or traumatic event around the time this started, such as a serious loss, accident, or threat to your safety? And if the work setback were to improve, do you think your symptoms would quickly improve too, or would they likely keep going?\n\nPatient: No, there weren't any other traumatic events or threats to my safety—the promotion setback was the main thing. And because this heavy sadness is so constant and doesn't lift even when good things happen, I feel like the symptoms would probably keep going even if my work situation got better."

Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
